For Opus, building a Babylon.js hex fleet tactics game (phone first, desktop tier, many ships on screen, faction readable effects, cinematic camera, UI clarity). This is the craft layer underneath the art direction: the timing, grading, readability and accessibility choices that make a game look expensive, most of which a non developer would never think to request. Each technique names its Babylon.js recipe where one exists, its phone cost, and a source. Where Babylon has no built in version of a technique, that is stated plainly instead of implied.

## 1. Colour and light craft

**Tone mapping: ACES versus the standard curve.** Tone mapping converts HDR light values into displayable colours, and the curve chosen is the single biggest determinant of whether a scene looks filmic or washed out. ACES is the film industry curve: it rolls off highlights smoothly, desaturates them gently, and keeps bright beams and explosions from clipping to flat white. The standard (Reinhard style) curve is brighter and flatter, and reads cheaper on dark space scenes. Babylon exposes ACES through the image processing configuration: `pipeline.imageProcessing.toneMappingEnabled = true` with `toneMappingType = BABYLON.ImageProcessingConfiguration.TONEMAPPING_ACES`, alongside contrast and vignette controls. Pick ACES for the cinematic tier and keep it consistent across regions, or every plate and LUT decision downstream gets rejudged.
Phone cost: tone mapping is a cheap fullscreen pass; the cost is in HDR rendering itself (see the pipeline trap in the tool guides: with hdr=true you must set `pipeline.samples` or lose anti-aliasing).
Source: https://www.webcodingcenter.com/babylon-js/post-processes-and-rendering-pipelines--a-post-processed-look-for-booknest-s-storefront-window.html

**Bloom threshold and knee.** Bloom should fire only on things that are actually bright: beams, engine glows, explosions, shield flashes. The two knobs are `bloomThreshold` (only pixels brighter than this bloom) and `bloomWeight` (how strongly the bloom is added back); a widely used starting pair is threshold 0.7 and weight 0.5. The knee is the soft rolloff around the threshold: a hard threshold makes bloom flicker on and off as a ship crosses the line, so the blur kernel (bloomScale and kernel size) is what softens it in practice. Rule of thumb for this game: raise the threshold until the background plates stop glowing, then tune weight until beams read without white-out.
Phone cost: bloom is a blur over the frame, so keep the kernel small on the phone tier and reserve wide blooms for desktop.
Source: https://dev.to/elysiatools/8-babylonjs-examples-that-prove-webgl-has-arrived-8a5

**Colour grading and LUTs.** A look up table (LUT) remaps every colour in the frame, which is how a whole region gets a mood (cold belt, hot storm, sickly graveyard) without touching a single material. This project's visual expansion research already established per-region .3dl LUTs as six tiny files; the craft point is authorship: grade in a photo tool (Photoshop, DaVinci Resolve) on a screenshot of the actual game, export the LUT, and judge it on the phone screen, not the monitor. A LUT that looks moody on a desktop monitor often reads as mud on a phone.
Phone cost: a LUT pass is nearly free; authoring badly costs readability, not frames.
Source: https://doc.babylonjs.com/features/featuresDeepDive/materials/shaders/image_processing

**Exposure adaptation.** Real eyes adapt to brightness, and many engines simulate it by auto adjusting exposure. In a tactics game, do not. Exposure shifts change which ships are readable from one turn to the next, and a player misreading a shield state because the exposure moved is a gameplay bug wearing a graphics feature. Fix exposure per region and let the LUT do the mood work.
Phone cost: none, this is a decision, not a pass.

**Fog and depth cueing.** Fog is not weather here, it is a depth cue: distant ships fade slightly toward the background colour so the eye reads near versus far at a glance. Babylon offers four fog modes: `scene.fogMode = BABYLON.Scene.FOGMODE_LINEAR` with `fogStart` and `fogEnd`, or `FOGMODE_EXP` and `FOGMODE_EXP2` with `fogDensity`, plus `fogColor`. In space, use a very light linear fog (or a distance desaturation in the grade) and never a thick fog: the board must stay readable at full zoom out, and fog that eats ship silhouettes is a readability failure.
Phone cost: fog is nearly free; it is evaluated per fragment, so keep it out of the particle shader path if one is custom.
Source: https://waelyasmina.net/articles/babylon-js-tutorial-for-absolute-beginners/

## 2. Anti-aliasing craft

**MSAA versus FXAA versus TAA, and the Babylon reality.** Three tools, three prices. MSAA samples geometry edges at render time: sharp, expensive, and in Babylon it is `pipeline.samples` (4 is the usual value) once HDR is on. FXAA is a post-process blur over jagged edges: cheap, always available via `pipeline.fxaaEnabled`, but it softens fine detail and text. TAA reuses previous frames to accumulate sub-pixel samples: the best quality in modern engines, and the one with a signature failure, ghosting. Babylon ships a TAARenderingPipeline, but it is a separate pipeline, not part of DefaultRenderingPipeline. The default pipeline offers MSAA (pipeline.samples) and FXAA (pipeline.fxaaEnabled) only. If TAA is wanted, create the TAA pipeline before other post-processes, or set reprojectHistory and clampHistory to tame ghosting. On a phone GPU, MSAA or FXAA remains the right default; the TAA notes below explain why that is a survivable loss and what other engines do when they do use it.
Phone cost: MSAA samples 4 on desktop, FXAA or samples 2 on phone. Never both MSAA and FXAA at once; the double blur buys nothing.
Source: https://forum.babylonjs.com/t/enabling-hdr-in-defaultpipeline-disables-aa/14656

**TAA ghosting and its mitigations (for understanding, not for building).** Ghosting is the temporal history failing to keep up with fast motion: a fast ship leaves a faint trail because the previous frame's pixels are reprojected to the wrong place. Engines mitigate it four ways, in order of importance: per-pixel motion vectors (velocity buffers) so the history is reprojected correctly, especially for materials that move without moving their mesh; neighborhood clamping, which rejects any history sample outside the colour range of the current frame's 3x3 neighbourhood; history rejection on large lighting changes; and higher frame rate, which makes ghosting visibly weaker because the history converges faster. Playdead's open source temporal anti-aliasing for INSIDE documents the full recipe (jitter, velocity buffer, reprojection, clamping, blend) and is the cleanest study source. For this game the actionable translation is: fast ships plus a trail effect will always read as ghosting to a player, so make trails deliberate (a designed TrailMesh or beam trail) rather than accidental.
Phone cost: TAA is a history buffer plus a velocity pass; on a phone GPU it is usually the wrong trade, which is another reason MSAA or FXAA is the right call here.
Sources: https://docs.godotengine.org/en/4.3/tutorials/3d/3d_antialiasing.html, https://deepwiki.com/playdeadgames/temporal, http://stevekarolewics.com/articles/anti-ghosting-taa.html

## 3. Motion and impact craft

**Anticipation, hit-stop, impact frames.** The three beats that make a hit feel heavy, from the game feel tradition (Jonasson and Purho's 2012 GDC talk is the origin point). Anticipation is a wind-up before the hit: a turret tracking, a charge glow building, 0.3 to 0.6 seconds. Hit-stop is a brief freeze at the moment of impact: 3 to 6 frames (0.05 to 0.1 seconds) where everything pauses, which the brain reads as weight. An impact frame is a 1 to 3 frame flash or white frame at the contact point. None of these change the simulation; all of them change whether the player believes it. In a turn based game they translate directly into animation beats: a volley is anticipation (track and charge), impact (flash, shake, hit-stop pause), aftermath (debris, smoke, damage numbers).
Phone cost: zero GPU cost; these are timing choices in the animation system. The risk is pacing: too much hit-stop in a 20 ship battle makes the game feel slow, so scale hit-stop by event size (capital kills get it, fighter pings do not).
Sources: https://www.manuelsanchezdev.com/blog/game-feel-principles-animated-godot, https://fishfolk.github.io/jumpy-legacy-docs/juice.html

**Screen shake, done right.** Shake is the most powerful and most abused juice technique. Good shake is short (0.1 to 0.3 seconds for ordinary hits, up to 0.5 for a capital kill), decays from its peak, scales with the event, has a directional bias away from the impact, and is built from smooth noise (Perlin or sine) rather than random offsets, which read as jarring. Bad shake lasts too long, stays at constant intensity, fires on every action, or moves the screen far enough to lose the board. Always ship a reduce or disable toggle; shake is a motion sickness vector, not just a preference.
Phone cost: none; it is a camera offset. The cost is readability, so never shake during the player's own aiming or targeting.
Source: https://www.slashskill.com/game-feel-and-juice-the-complete-guide-to-making-your-game-satisfying/

**Camera push-ins and hit flashes.** A slow push-in (about 1.2 seconds) on a Command activation or a boss arrival tells the player "this matters" before the event resolves; the project's cinematic section already uses this timing. Hit flashes are the damage feedback counterpart: the struck ship flashes white for a frame or two, and the health bar sells the hit by dropping the fill immediately but draining a ghost bar (often orange) a beat later, so the eye catches the damage even when it is watching the target, not the HUD.
Phone cost: camera moves are free; the ghost bar is a UI animation, not a render cost.
Source: https://www.youtube.com/shorts/LeTA_m0ABGg

## 4. Readability systems

**Silhouette, rim light, outlines.** At full zoom out, a ship is a handful of pixels, and colour alone cannot carry identity. Three layers, in order of cheapness: shape language (each class reads by outline alone: wedge, ring, fin, cathedral), rim light (a Fresnel term that brightens edges facing away from the camera; in Babylon this is a Node Material fresnel block, or a HighlightLayer), and outlines (Babylon's HighlightLayer draws a coloured halo around chosen meshes using the stencil buffer, so the engine must be created with `{ stencil: true }`, then `new BABYLON.HighlightLayer('outline', scene).addMesh(mesh, color)`; `outerGlow` and `innerGlow` plus blur sizes tune it, and a newer SelectionOutlineLayer draws a crisp editor style outline). Use outlines sparingly: selection and targeting only. If every ship has an outline, no ship does.
Phone cost: HighlightLayer costs extra blurred render targets; reserve it for the selected and targeted ships, not the fleet.
Sources: https://www.webcodingcenter.com/babylon-js/post-processes-and-rendering-pipelines--the-glow-layer-and-highlight-layer.html, https://www.webcodingcenter.com/babylon-js/the-node-material-editor--a-node-material-spine-highlight-for-booknest-s-shelves.html

**Contrast budgets.** The project's existing rule (no background pixel inside the board brighter than 40 percent of an unlit hull facet) is the right shape: a contrast budget, stated as a number, enforced per region. Extend it: the faintest ship colour against the brightest background pixel in its region must clear a stated ratio, checked on a phone screenshot, not a monitor. Faction identity must also survive colour loss: never encode meaning with colour alone, which is the bridge to section 8.

## 5. LOD and streaming craft

**LOD levels and popping.** Babylon's `mesh.addLODLevel(distance, simplerMesh)` swaps in a lighter mesh beyond a distance, and `addLODLevel(distance, null)` stops drawing entirely; `mesh.useLODScreenCoverage = true` makes thresholds screen fractions (0 to 1) instead of distances, which is scale independent and usually the better choice for a game with ships of very different sizes. Instances inherit their source's levels. Build the lighter meshes offline (Blender Decimate, glTF-Transform simplify). Babylon has no built in LOD crossfade, so popping is mitigated three ways: pick thresholds where the silhouette barely changes, add hysteresis (different thresholds for zooming in versus out, so the mesh does not flicker at the boundary), or fade opacity briefly across the swap on the few hero ships where popping would be noticed.
Phone cost: LOD is a pure win; the cost is authoring time and the memory of holding two meshes per hull, which the phone tier already budgets for.
Sources: https://www.webcodingcenter.com/babylon-js/rendering-many-objects-efficiently--level-of-detail-with-addlodlevel.html, https://forum.babylonjs.com/t/some-question-about-using-lod-to-simplify-mesh/56598

**Texture streaming and mip bias.** The phone tier does not need 4K textures on a ship that covers 40 pixels. The practical rules: ship textures at the resolution the zoom-out view actually shows, anisotropy capped on mobile, and KTX2 compression (already in the tool guides) doing the heavy lifting. Mip bias (forcing a sharper or blurrier mip level) is a tuning knob for shimmering versus blur on distant ships; use it only if distant hulls visibly shimmer, and prefer fixing the texture (less high frequency detail) over biasing the sampler.

**Particle overdraw budgets.** Overdraw is the same pixel shaded many times because transparent particles overlap, and it is the classic phone killer: a particle system that is cheap in isolation becomes devastating when twenty explosions overlap. The diagnostic is simple: if frame rate drops when looking directly at effects and recovers when zooming out so the particles occupy fewer pixels, the bottleneck is fill rate, not particle count. The rules: trim transparent borders off particle textures (empty pixels still cost shading), prefer additive blending for sparks and fire (cheaper than alpha blending), avoid large layers of faint smoke, cap simultaneous systems per family, and treat an overdraw ratio consistently above about 2.0 (every pixel shaded twice) as the budget line, per mobile GPU guidance. Measure with the engine's profiling tools and with Spector.js frame captures, not by feel.
Phone cost: this is the phone cost. Overdraw is the first thing to cut on the phone tier, before particle counts.
Sources: https://bugnet.io/blog/debugging-particle-system-performance-issues, https://blog.imaginationtech.com/improving-particle-systems-on-powervr/, https://ozlin.info/2026/03/implementing-particle-systems-for-visual-effects/

## 6. UI motion craft

**Easing and duration.** The rules that separate polished UI from robotic UI: entrances ease out (fast start, soft landing), exits ease in, paired elements share easing and duration, durations stay under 300 ms (micro interactions 100 to 150 ms, standard controls 150 to 250 ms, modals and drawers 200 to 300 ms), and linear motion is reserved for continuous loops like spinners. A good default curve is cubic-bezier(0.4, 0, 0.2, 1). Animate transform and opacity only; animating layout properties (width, top, left) forces re-layout every frame and stutters on phones. Every animation must be interruptible: a player tapping twice should not lock the UI until the first animation finishes.
Phone cost: transform and opacity animations are compositor cheap; layout animations are not.
Sources: https://gist.github.com/uxderrick/07b81ca63932865ef1a7dc94fbe07838, https://namethatui.com/web/easing

**Feedback timing and HUD fade.** A button must acknowledge a press within about 100 ms or it feels dead, even if the action itself takes longer; the acknowledgement can be a scale dip, a sound, or both. During big events (capital kills, boss arrivals), fade the HUD out briefly so the cinematic moment owns the screen, then bring it back; the project's Sins-derived section already uses this, and the craft point is that the fade is what makes the moment feel directed rather than just loud.
Phone cost: none beyond the animations themselves.

## 7. Audio as effects craft

**Ducking.** When an important sound plays, everything else dips. In middleware this is sidechain compression (one bus's level drives another bus's gain); in a web game without middleware, implement it as explicit gain automation: on a flagship kill or a stinger, duck music by 6 to 12 dB for the duration, then release smoothly. The priority order is voice first, then gameplay-critical effects, then music last. Ducking is what keeps a big explosion from burying the sound that tells the player what just happened.
Phone cost: none; it is gain math.
Sources: https://qa.fmod.com/t/ducking-background-music-for-other-audio/13095, https://www.audiokinetic.com/en/courses/wwise201/?source=wwise201&id=lesson_8_mixing_side_chaining_stingers

**Stingers and adaptive music.** A stinger is a short musical phrase triggered by an event (boss arrival, flagship kill, victory) that plays regardless of the music underneath; sidechaining the music under it is what guarantees it is heard. Adaptive music has two mechanisms: vertical layering (one piece written as stems, drums, bass, brass, added or removed as intensity rises and falls, all sharing tempo and key so changes feel natural) and horizontal resequencing (the music jumps between sections at bar or beat boundaries, through a short transition, for bigger mood changes). For a tactics game, vertical layering maps cleanly onto battle intensity (add percussion when the enemy commits, brass when the boss arrives), and resequencing maps onto campaign state (menu, battle, victory). A tactics game needs very little music; it needs the right three seconds at the right moment.
Phone cost: stems cost memory, not CPU; keep the stem count small on the phone tier.
Source: http://dev.to/aplusgamesstudio/video-game-music-how-it-is-composed-and-implemented-1969

**UI sound principles.** Every action gets a sound, sounds vary slightly per play (about 5 to 10 percent pitch variation) so repetition does not grate, and quiet moments are protected: if everything is loud, nothing is. UI sounds are feedback, not decoration, and a silent button is a broken button to the player's ear.

## 8. Accessibility craft

**Colorblind safe palettes.** Start from the Okabe-Ito palette, the standard colorblind safe categorical set (also published as the Wong palette): black #000000, orange #E69F00, sky blue #56B4E9, bluish green #009E73, yellow #F0E442, blue #0072B2, vermillion #D55E00, reddish purple #CC79A7. Two habits make any palette safer: never encode meaning with red versus green alone (pair colour with a second channel: shape, icon, label, position), and test with both a grayscale check (which tests luminance contrast) and a colour vision deficiency simulation for protanopia, deuteranopia and tritanopia (grayscale alone does not replace simulation). Note the trap in the set: only black and blue reach 4.5:1 contrast against white, and yellow is especially weak, so keep labels dark and test marks against their actual backgrounds. For this game the actionable version is: faction colours must be distinguishable under deuteranopia simulation, and faction identity must also be readable by silhouette and icon when colour is removed entirely.
Phone cost: none.
Sources: https://conceptviz.app/blog/scientific-color-palette-for-research-papers-and-posters, https://scifig.ai/blog/color-palettes-scientific-figures, https://www.audioeye.com/post/colorblind-friendly-palettes/

**Photosensitivity limits.** WCAG's seizures guideline is a hard safety rule, not a nicety: content must not flash more than three times in any one-second period (Level A allows flashes below the general and red flash thresholds; Level AAA requires no more than three flashes per second outright, and technique G19 recommends designing to at most three as the easily testable rule). Red flashes are the most dangerous colour. Practical rules for this game: cap screen flashes at three per second across all simultaneous effects, keep any flashing area small, never strobe a full-screen flash for a capital kill, and ship a reduce-flash setting that caps flash brightness and rate. Test with the Trace Center Photosensitive Epilepsy Analysis Tool (PEAT) or a Harding test where PEAT is restricted.
Phone cost: none; this is a design constraint on effects.
Sources: https://www.w3.org/WAI/GL/2013/WD-WCAG20-TECHS-20130117/G19, https://case.edu/accessibility/what-accessibility/guidelines/flashing-content, https://adobe.design/ideas/improving-product-accessibility-for-users-with-epilepsy

**Motion reduction.** Respect the platform's reduced-motion preference and ship an in-game toggle that disables or damps screen shake, camera push-ins, background animation and idle bobbing. Vestibular disorders and migraines are triggered by exactly the motion that makes a game feel alive to everyone else, so the toggle is not a niche feature; it is the difference between playable and unplayable for part of the audience. The toggle must also kill auto-playing background motion, not just shake.
Phone cost: none.

## What was not verified

Babylon's exact tone mapping type names beyond TONEMAPPING_ACES (shown in the linked example) were not re-opened against the official docs in this pass; confirm the standard curve constant against the installed Babylon version before wiring it. Babylon has a built-in TAA rendering pipeline (TAARenderingPipeline), separate from DefaultRenderingPipeline; the earlier 'not found' note is superseded. The .3dl LUT workflow is established in this project's own visual expansion research rather than re-verified against Babylon docs in this pass. Forum and third-party guides (webcodingcenter, tutorialspoint) are used for several Babylon recipes; treat those as community practice, and prefer the official docs where they cover the same setting.


===== FILE: beauty_corner_spec.md =====

# Beauty corner test scene: specification

Status: specification, not a task order and not a schedule. Opus may use this scene, change it, or set it aside. Its purpose is narrow: prove one of each thing at target frame rate, in a scene separate from the game, before that thing is integrated into the game. Nothing in this spec assigns implementation sequence or dates.

Basis: art_direction_rey.md (colour laws, silhouette grammars, hero concepts, plate system, effects language), ship_kit_upgrade.md (kit pieces, budgets, gates, acceptance tests), visual_expansion_research.md (cinematic recipes, pipeline settings), tool_guides.md (Blender export, Babylon performance, gltfpack tiers, KTX2, Spector.js), graphics_craft.md (tonemapping, bloom discipline, anti-aliasing, VFX timing, readability, overdraw, accessibility). Where those documents disagree, section 7 flags it instead of picking a winner.

## 1. Scene contents

The corner is one Babylon scene, loadable on its own, with no campaign, no turns, and no game logic. Everything in it is scripted: fixed camera paths, fixed ship placements, fixed effect triggers, so two runs are comparable.

### Hulls: six heroes, one per faction

- Voidsworn: Basilica, carrier. Twin spire towers flanking an open launch nave, stained-glass hangar mouths glowing amber.
- Hegemony: Edict, battlecruiser. The perfect wedge, three stepped decks, ion batteries in recessed centreline trenches, sensor dome on the centreline.
- Corsair: Jackdaw, torpedo skiff. Half a stolen picket welded to an oversized engine block, external torpedo rack bigger than the crew hull, mandatory asymmetry.
- Chorus: Lullaby, spore carrier. Gravid brood sac, translucent spore pods clustered under chitin plates, radial knot, no straight edge longer than a plate.
- Ithar: Palinode, sail cruiser. Needle hull, twin vertical sail fins (one dorsal, one ventral) lit jade from behind, gold filigree spars. Vertical fins only; horizontal sail wings are banned.
- Deathless: Cenotaph, tomb carrier. A ribcage at station scale, bone prow plate blank and staring, green light deep in the ribs.

Each hull appears twice in the scene: once lit in its home plate, once on the silhouette sheet (section 5). The six hero plates in img/ (ship_voidsworn_basilica.webp, ship_hegemony_edict.webp, ship_corsair_jackdaw.webp, ship_chorus_lullaby.webp, ship_ithar_palinode.webp, ship_deathless_cenotaph.webp) are the reference targets for the lit shots.

### Background plates: six anchors

One plate per terrain kind, so the corner proves the plate system against the game's actual terrain set: Belt, Storm, Graveyard, Planet, Station, Dead Star. Each plate is built as the four layers from the art direction: far gradient nebula (two colours max, region tinted), one mid feature, near particulate matched to the feature, starfield with per-plate density and colour temperature. The 12 variant and special plates (Belt Core, Crimson Storm, Stellar Nursery and the rest) are out of scope for the first version of the corner, except Stellar Nursery, which serves as the backdrop for the capital event shot in section 2. The plate-to-terrain mapping gap is flagged in section 7; the corner proves plates, it does not settle the mapping.

### Lighting rig

- One key light, the system primary, warm from one side, consistent across all six plates so hulls are comparable.
- Faction glow lights: ember orange (Voidsworn), clean blue (Hegemony), hot red with flicker (Corsair), bile (Chorus), soft jade (Ithar), terminal green (Deathless). Glow is set dressing and engine light, never shield colour.
- Ambient: low, cold, uniform. No fill light strong enough to flatten a silhouette.
- Grading: ACES tone mapping, one fixed exposure per plate (never auto-adapted exposure; an exposure shift that changes which ships read is a gameplay bug, per graphics_craft.md). Bloom threshold and weight are test variables (section 7), starting at threshold 0.7 and weight 0.5.
- Colour source: FAC3D is the single colour source. If a hull in the corner disagrees with FAC3D, the corner is wrong, not the table.

### Cameras and shot list

- Fleet view: the full board framing, the readability test distance. Every faction readability check in section 5 is judged here.
- Hero framing: the boss arrival framing (radius from 1.6x fleet view to hero framing, 3.5 second move, BezierCurveEase biased to a slow finish, letterbox bars in over the first 0.4 seconds).
- Command push-in: 1.2 second push-in of about 15 percent radius on the commanding ship, ease-out, brief bloom weight lift, then release. No letterbox.
- Targeting close-up: single ship, prow quarter, for shield ripple and damage state inspection.
- Silhouette sheet camera: orthographic flat black grid, all six heroes small, for the sheet test in section 5.

## 2. Effects to prove, one by one

Each effect is proven separately, in isolation, before any combined shot. An effect that only works when it is the only thing on screen has not passed.

- **Beam fire.** Stretched additive cylinder or ribbon between muzzle and target, bright core plus soft shell at 1.6x diameter and about 0.25 alpha, noise texture scrolling along the core so the beam boils. Fade in over 40 ms, out over 80 ms. Muzzle flash sprite and impact glow sprite with sub-emitter sparks. Cap: 12 concurrent beams. Faction character per the art direction: Voidsworn lances charge with a rising ember flare; Hegemony ion fire is instant and clinical; Ithar beams are hair-thin and blinding at the core; Deathless gauss fire is a green line that arrives before its glow.
- **Shield ripple and per-facing shield death.** All factions, ice cyan only, per the colour law. Faction identity is carried by ripple shape only: Hegemony perfect hexagons, Voidsworn arch segments, Chorus blistered, Ithar smooth gradient, Deathless cracked glass, Corsair patchy and flickering. Two sub-tests: a ripple on a full shield, and a facing death where one sector dies while the others hold, with the dead sector protecting nothing until restarted.
- **Explosion.** One per faction, following the art direction death signatures: Voidsworn breaks like a burning building with spires toppling inward; Hegemony dies in one white flash and clean halves; Corsairs come apart at the welds with donor sections spinning away intact; Chorus deflates and darkens with pods bursting late; Ithar shatters like glass, sails first; Deathless goes dark in segments, stern to prow, green lights last out.
- **Engine trail.** Deliberate TrailMesh or beam trail, never accidental motion blur. Trails fade by camera distance and fighter trails are dropped entirely at fleet view. Trail screen area is budgeted, not trail count.
- **Torpedo flight.** Multi-turn travel with limited turning, so the salvo herds movement even when it misses. Corsair torpedoes burn dirty and wobble. The corner proves the visual (a slow, visible, turning threat) rather than the game rule.
- **Boarding flash.** The boarding action as a visible event: fires starting on the target hull plus an engine cripple read (dimmed engine glow, slowed plume) for one round. Range, cooldown and consequences belong to the game; the corner proves the read.
- **Capital event.** The full directed beat, in order: HUD fade out, letterbox bars in over 0.4 seconds, 3.5 second arrival move to hero framing, depth of field enabled only for this shot on desktop (dropped on phone, where the move and letterbox carry the shot), bloom weight lift, then release and HUD fade back in. This is the only shot in the corner allowed to use depth of field.
- **Damage states.** One per faction, per the art direction: Voidsworn cracks glowing ember from inside; Hegemony venting blue coolant in clean jets; Corsairs shedding rust and a donor panel; Chorus bleeding bile with the wound knitting visibly; Ithar sails tearing and going dark panel by panel; Deathless losing a rib segment that drifts, then burning green and flying home on reassembly.

## 3. Budget gates per tier

All figures below come from the design documents. Where a document gives an observed range rather than a ceiling, the gate is marked provisional and the corner's job includes turning it into a measured ceiling.

### Phone tier

Dropped under the Oct 24, 2026 PC-only ruling. There is no phone tier, no phone gate, and no phone shot in this spec. The desktop tier below is the only tier.

### Desktop tier

- Frame rate: sustained 60 fps over the same 60 second scripted run, with the desktop pipeline enabled.
- Draw calls: under 1,000 for the full corner scene at fleet view (community guidance: roughly 500 to 1,000 on desktop browsers at 60 fps).
- Hull triangles: per hull within the observed desktop range of 2,400 to 38,000 triangles. All 36 desktop hulls total 930,166 triangles; the corner's six heroes are a subset and must stay within the same per-hull range.
- Texture memory: KTX2 throughout; the desktop ceiling is set by measurement in the corner, not by this spec, because the documents give no desktop VRAM ceiling. Record it; do not guess it.
- Pipeline on desktop: MSAA 4, full bloom, depth of field allowed on the capital event shot only, LUT grade, skybox stack, camera easing.

### Cut order (both tiers, from the research memo)

When the target frame rate cannot be held, cut in this order: fighter trails at distance, shell layers on beams, GlowLayer, bloom weight, then MSAA samples. Never cut the LUT grade, the skybox stack, or camera easing. The corner records which cut was needed, if any, per effect: a gate passed only by cutting is a conditional pass, not a pass.

### What fails the gate

Any of: average frame rate below the tier target; a background pixel inside the board area above the 40 percent luminance cap; a hull that fails the silhouette sheet test; a shield effect that uses any colour other than ice cyan; an effect that only works in isolation and collapses in the combined shot; a per-hull triangle count above its tier range; an uncompressed texture. A conditional pass (gate met only after a cut from the cut order) is recorded as conditional, with the cut named.

## 4. Measurement procedure

Comparability is the whole point. Two runs that cannot be compared are two anecdotes.

- **Scripted run.** One fixed 60 second sequence: fleet view hold (10 seconds), hero arrival (3.5 seconds plus hold), Command push-in (1.2 seconds plus hold), targeting close-up per hull (5 seconds each), one beam volley, one shield ripple per faction, one facing death, one explosion per faction, one torpedo flight, one boarding flash, one capital event, damage state per faction, silhouette sheet hold (10 seconds). Same order, same camera paths, every run.
- **Frame rate and frame time.** Inspector Statistics tab: frames per second, draw calls, active meshes. Record average fps, 1 percent low fps, and the worst 5 second window, per tier, per run. Record device (model, OS version), build identifier, and whether the device was thermally warm (note it; do not control for it beyond recording it).
- **Spector.js capture.** One capture per tier per run, on the combined shot (all six heroes, one plate, one beam volley, one explosion). Steps: open the corner, start Spector capture, run the combined shot, stop capture, inspect the command list for hidden passes and forgotten render targets, and record pass count and any unexpected render target. Spector.js is MIT licensed and lives in the Babylon.js GitHub organization.
- **Run record.** One short text file per run, kept beside the corner: date, device, tier, build identifier, average fps, 1 percent low, worst 5 second window, draw calls at fleet view, triangle counts per hull, texture memory estimate, which cuts (if any) were needed, and the screenshot set from section 5. A run without its record does not count.
- **Regression rule.** A later run that is slower than the recorded baseline by more than 10 percent on any headline number (average fps, draw calls, texture memory) is a regression to investigate, not noise to ignore.

## 5. Acceptance criteria

- **Screenshot comparisons.** Every lit hull shot is compared side by side with its concept plate (same angle and lighting where the plate allows it). Pass is judged by eye against the plate: silhouette grammar present, faction palette present, no flat single-colour read. The comparison screenshots are kept with the run record.
- **Readability checks.** At fleet view, each hull must be identifiable as its faction by silhouette and icon without relying on colour. Test method: the fleet view screenshot in grayscale; if two factions merge in grayscale, the pair fails and the distinguishing feature (geometry, icon, trim pattern) must be strengthened. Colour is never the only channel carrying faction identity.
- **Silhouette sheet test (from the kit doc).** All six heroes rendered flat black, small, in a grid. Any two hulls a viewer cannot tell apart, or any hull a viewer assigns to the wrong faction, sends the hull back. This test is run at desktop size. Phone-size testing is dropped with the phone target.
- **40 percent background luminance cap check.** Method: render one unlit hull facet as a reference swatch, measure its luminance, then sample background pixels inside the board area across all six anchor plates. No sampled pixel may exceed 40 percent of the reference luminance. Procedure: sample at least 20 points per plate (corners, centre, brightest visible feature, plate edges), record the maximum per plate, and fail any plate over the cap regardless of beauty. Region tinting (a whisper of home faction glow in the far gradient) is allowed only inside the cap.
- **Shield colour check.** Sample the shield ripple pixels in the targeting close-up: ice cyan family only, all factions. Any faction-coloured shield pixel fails.
- **Gold check.** Sample gold pixels in the lit shots: gold may appear only on patrons and Command UI. Gold or gold-adjacent trim on a hull fails, subject to the open gold question in section 7.
- **Accessibility checks (from graphics_craft.md).** No more than three flashes per second across all simultaneous effects; no full-screen strobe for a capital kill; a reduce-flash setting exists and caps flash brightness and rate. Faction colours must be distinguishable under deuteranopia simulation, and faction identity must survive colour removal entirely (see the grayscale test above).

## 6. How the scene relates to the game without being part of it

The corner is a harness, not a branch of the game. It shares the game's materials, pipeline settings and effect parameters by copy, not by import, so that proving something in the corner does not accidentally change the game, and breaking the game does not break the corner.

What copies over when an item passes: the validated material and pipeline settings, the effect parameters (timings, thresholds, caps), the LOD thresholds, the measured budgets, and the reference screenshots. What stays in the corner: the scripted camera paths, the reference swatches, the measurement rig, the run records, and any test-only geometry (the silhouette sheet grid, the unlit facet swatch).

The corner does not replace the kit doc's desktop stress gate: no hull 6 until a fully loaded battle passes on desktop. The corner is the step before that gate, where individual items are proven cheap enough to deserve a place in the battle that the gate measures.

## 7. Disagreements flagged, not resolved

- **MSAA samples on phone.** Moot under the Oct 24, 2026 PC-only ruling: phone settings no longer apply. Desktop acceptance uses MSAA 4.
- **Bloom threshold.** The research memo uses a bloom threshold around 0.85 for beams; graphics_craft starts at threshold 0.7 and weight 0.5. The corner starts at 0.7 and 0.5 and raises the threshold until the background plates stop glowing, recording the chosen pair per tier.
- **Gold on hulls.** The art direction rations gold to patrons and Command only, but Chorus trim is dull gold, Ithar trim is pale gold, and epic rarity stays gold-adjacent. The gold check in section 5 will fail hulls that follow the art direction's own trim values. This is an open design question (raised by the design council audit), not a corner failure: record the failing pixels, do not silently recolour the hulls to pass.
- **Ripple shape as the only shield channel.** The art direction carries shield identity in ripple shape only, while the same document set admits glow is the first thing lost at small size. The fleet view readability check in section 5 is the test that decides whether shape alone survives; if it does not, the shield language needs a second channel, and that is a design decision, not a corner fix.
- **Plate mapping.** 18 plates exist against five terrain kinds with no mapping in any document. The corner proves the six anchor plates; it does not invent the mapping. Until a mapping exists, the corner's plate shots are labelled by plate name, not by terrain kind.
- **Kit economics.** The kit doc's 69-session figure was the hand-built kit rate. The corner measures dressing, paint and format conversion of existing meshes (see the reading plan errata), not hull creation from nothing. Any cost claim from the corner must state which of those it measured.

## 8. Reference scoring (added Oct 24, 2026, from a technique Ryan flagged)

Score the render against the reference instead of eyeballing it alone. For every lit hull shot and plate shot in section 5, run a programmatic comparison against the matching concept plate: palette and histogram match, light and contrast match, and the 40 percent background luminance cap measurement from section 5. Record the score with the run record.

The score is a tone and lighting gate only. It cannot pass a shot on its own. Silhouette readability, faction identification at fleet zoom, and the owner's screenshot verdict are separate gates, and a high score on a wrong-looking ship is still a fail. The loop discipline that goes with it: change one thing per iteration, keep a side-by-side of render versus reference for each change, and record the change note with the run record.

Auto-fitting a color grade (LUT) to the reference plate as a final step is a legitimate tool, not a cheat, provided the fitted grade is recorded with the shot and the per-region LUT set stays a versioned artifact, not a silent tweak.

## 9. Platform note (Oct 24, 2026 ruling: PC-only)

This spec was first written phone-first and was re-tiered under Ryan's Oct 24, 2026 platform ruling: PC-only in Babylon, phone target dropped entirely. The desktop tier is the only tier. The phone tier section, phone gates, phone shots, and phone settings in earlier drafts are struck; where this file still mentions phone figures, they are historical context, not gates. The desktop battle pass is the gate: no hull 6 until a fully loaded battle passes on desktop. The engine stays Babylon; a wrapper or engine migration is a separate, undecided question.


===== FILE: visual_qa_pack.md =====

# Visual QA pack: screenshot specification for Voidsworn

Status: specification Opus may use or set aside. It is not a task order and it sets no sequence or dates. It exists because the owner judges visual work by screenshots, so the screenshots themselves need a standard: same conditions, same shots, same checks, same verdict record, every time.

Rules this pack enforces come from the existing design documents, not from new invention:

- Colour law (art direction): cyan means shields, nothing else is cyan, and shields are nothing else. Gold is rationed to patrons and Command UI only. Selection ring is white. Flagship marker is a faction-coloured chevron. FAC3D is the single colour source.
- Silhouette grammars (art direction): Voidsworn vertical cathedral, Hegemony pure wedge, Corsair mandatory asymmetry, Chorus radial knot with no straight edges, Ithar dart plus vertical fins (never horizontal wings), Deathless ribcage and spine.
- Background cap (art direction): no background pixel inside the board area may exceed 40 percent of the luminance of an unlit hull facet.
- Kit acceptance (ship kit work order): two shots per hull at battle zoom on a desktop frame, one lit against its home plate, one flat-black silhouette sheet. A hull that cannot be told apart from another hull of its faction, or that a viewer assigns to the wrong faction, sends the manifest back.
- Craft rules (graphics craft): fixed exposure per region, never auto-adapted; bloom threshold raised until background plates stop glowing; MSAA on desktop; trails deliberate, never accidental; screen shake short with a reduce toggle; photosensitivity cap of three flashes per second; a reduce-flash setting.

## 1. Capture setup: identical conditions, every batch

A screenshot that cannot be compared to the last batch is decoration. Before any shot in a batch:

- Wait for shader compilation to finish. Force synchronous compilation before a screenshot, or wait on scene readiness, so no shot catches a half-compiled material.
- Fix exposure per region. Never capture with auto exposure enabled: an exposure shift that changes which ships read is a gameplay bug, and it also makes two batches incomparable.
- Use the production pipeline settings for the tier being shot: ACES tone mapping on, bloom at the shipped threshold and weight, the desktop anti-aliasing mode (MSAA samples).
- Capture the desktop frame for every subject. Under the Oct 24, 2026 PC-only ruling there is no phone frame and no phone acceptance check. A shot passes or fails on desktop alone.
- Capture in game, never in Blender. A Blender render is not proof. The in-game screenshot and the flat-black silhouette sheet are the acceptance tests.
- Record the build identifier and the tier with the batch. A shot without a build tag cannot be re-checked.

## 2. Screenshot capture list

### 2a. Per hull (every hull, every tier)

1. Lit hero: hull at battle zoom, lit, against its home plate, three-quarter view, engines on. This is the kit acceptance shot (a).
2. Silhouette sheet: the hull rendered flat black, small, in a grid with every other hull of its faction. This is the kit acceptance shot (b).
3. Top-down board view: the hull on the hex board at the zoom the player actually fights at, beside one same-class hull of a different faction.
4. Damage states: the hull at heavy damage and at near destruction, showing its faction damage language (Voidsworn ember cracks, Hegemony blue coolant jets, Corsair shed donor panel, Chorus bile and knitting wound, Ithar dark sail panels, Deathless lost rib segment).
5. Shield hit: the hull taking a hit, showing the cyan ripple in its faction shape (Hegemony hexagons, Voidsworn arch segments, Chorus blistered, Ithar smooth gradient, Deathless cracked glass, Corsair patchy and flickering).
6. Weapon fire: the hull firing its signature weapon, showing muzzle character and projectile or beam colour.
7. Engine view: stern view, engines running, showing plume character (Corsair dirty flicker versus Hegemony clean, as specified).

### 2b. Per effect (every effect family, both tiers)

1. Beam: full travel from muzzle to impact, one frame mid-flight and one frame at impact.
2. Shield hit: per faction, as in 2a.5, plus one frame showing the ripple after the hit fades.
3. Explosion and destruction: the full destruction sequence for one hull per faction, showing that no two factions die alike (Voidsworn burning inward, Hegemony white flash and clean halves, Corsair coming apart at the welds, Chorus deflating with late pod bursts, Ithar shattering sails first, Deathless going dark stern to prow).
4. Engine trail: a ship under full movement, showing the trail as a deliberate designed element, not a smear.
5. Muzzle flash and charge: a Voidsworn lance charging (rising ember flare) and a Hegemony ion shot firing instantly, side by side in the batch, so the timing contrast is visible.
6. Simultaneous effects: a frame with at least three effect families active at once, for the overdraw and flash checks.

### 2c. Per background plate (every plate, both tiers)

1. Plate alone: the full plate with no ships and no UI, so the four layers read cleanly.
2. Plate with ships: the same plate with one fleet of each of two factions on the board, at fighting zoom, for the luminance cap check.
3. Plate with UI: the same plate with the full HUD visible, for the legibility check.
4. Plate at phone width: dropped. Under the Oct 24, 2026 PC-only ruling there is no phone frame; the plate with ships shot is captured on desktop only.

### 2d. Full battle (one battle per batch, both tiers)

1. Turn start: the board at rest, both fleets visible, HUD visible.
2. Mid-volley: at least three ships firing, shields hit, damage numbers visible.
3. Capital kill: the kill frame and one frame after, showing destruction, HUD fade behaviour, and flash count.
4. Command activation: a Command firing, showing the camera push-in and the Command UI state.
5. Victory and defeat: the end screens, showing that HUD and backdrop still read after the battle state changes.
6. Boss arrival: if the batch includes a boss, the arrival frame showing the push-in and the boss read.

## 3. Per-shot checklists

Check every applicable item on every shot. A check is either observed or it is not; do not mark a check from memory of a previous batch.

### All shots

- The shot was taken in game, at the shipped pipeline settings, with exposure fixed, after shader compilation finished.
- Nothing in the shot is a placeholder, a stand-in, or a superseded asset (no aft-pointing Confessor lance, no wet-navy battleship defender, no superseded portrait version).
- The build tag and tier are recorded with the shot.

### Hull shots

- Silhouette: the hull reads as its faction from outline alone, in flat black, at the size it appears in game. If faction and class do not read from the silhouette alone, the hull fails regardless of texture quality.
- Faction at a glance: on the lit hero shot, a viewer who has never seen the hull names the faction in under three seconds.
- Colour law: no cyan anywhere except shields, shield hits and shield UI pips. No gold anywhere except patrons and Command UI. Selection ring is white. Flagship marker is a faction-coloured chevron.
- Grammar: the hull obeys its faction grammar (Voidsworn vertical, Hegemony wedge line unbroken, Corsair asymmetrical, Chorus no straight edge longer than a plate, Ithar fins vertical, Deathless ribs exposed). Guns face forward or sideways, never backward.
- Damage language: the damage state shown matches the faction's specified language, and damage is readable at fighting zoom, not only at hero zoom.
- Shield shape: the ripple shape matches the faction, and the ripple reads at battle zoom.

### Effect shots

- Faction character: the effect matches its specified muzzle, projectile and timing character (Voidsworn slow ember charge, Hegemony instant and clinical, Corsair dirty wobble, Chorus pulsing globs, Ithar hair-thin beam, Deathless silent green line).
- Bloom sanity: beams, engine glows, explosions and shield flashes bloom. Background plates do not bloom. Nothing clips to flat white except a Hegemony death flash, which is specified to do so.
- Trails: every trail visible is a deliberate designed trail. No accidental smear reads as motion ghosting.
- Overdraw: in the simultaneous effects shot, frame rate holds when looking directly at the effects. If it drops when effects fill the screen and recovers when zooming out, the batch is flagged for fill rate, not particle count.
- Flash count: no more than three flashes in any one-second window across all simultaneous effects, and no full-screen strobe for a capital kill. The reduce-flash setting, if present, is shown working in a second capture of the same event.

### Background plate shots

- Luminance cap: no background pixel inside the board area exceeds 40 percent of the luminance of an unlit hull facet. Check by sampling, not by eye alone: sample the brightest board-area pixel against an unlit facet in the same shot.
- Ship separation: the faintest ship colour in the shot still reads against the brightest background pixel in its region, on the desktop frame.
- Layer discipline: the four layers read (far gradient, one mid feature, near particulate, starfield), and the mid feature does not compete with the ships for attention.
- Region seasoning: where a plate is seasoned for a home faction, the tint is a whisper, not a repaint. A Hegemony belt and a Deathless belt are cousins, not clones.
- UI legibility: with the HUD visible, every HUD element reads against the plate. If any element needs a scrim or a colour change, that is a finding, not a styling preference.

### Battle shots

- Board readability at fighting zoom: every ship's faction and rough damage state read without selecting it.
- HUD behaviour: the HUD fades during capital kills and boss arrivals and returns cleanly. No HUD element is lost, duplicated or left faded after the event.
- Camera behaviour: push-ins are slow (about 1.2 seconds for a Command or boss arrival) and never fire during the player's own aiming or targeting. Shake is short, decays, scales with the event, and is absent during targeting.
- UI motion: button presses acknowledge within about 100 ms. Animations run under 300 ms and are interruptible. No layout animation stutters the frame.
- Accessibility: the reduce-flash setting and the motion reduction toggle, if present, are each shown working in a second capture of the same event.

### Known tensions: check explicitly, do not smooth over

These are open contradictions in the design documents. A batch must report on them rather than assume them resolved:

- Gold-adjacent trims (Chorus dull gold, Ithar pale gold, epic rarity) must not read as Command gold. If a viewer confuses a trim for a Command marker, that is a finding against the rationing rule, not against the viewer.
- Shield identity is carried by ripple shape, while the same documents admit glow is the first thing lost at small size. The ripple shape check must therefore be done at battle zoom.
- Plate to terrain mapping is unmapped (18 plates against five terrain kinds). Until a mapping exists, every plate shot must record which terrain kind it is claimed for, so the mapping can be built from evidence rather than asserted.

## 4. Scoring rubric

Every shot in a batch gets one grade. Grades apply to shots, not to batches: a batch can contain passes and fails at once.

- **Pass.** Every applicable check observed. The shot goes to the owner verdict loop as approved material.
- **Pass with notes.** Every load-bearing check observed (silhouette, faction at a glance, colour law, luminance cap, flash count), with one or more minor notes attached (a bloom weight to tune, a UI element that wants a scrim, a trail that wants shortening). Notes do not block the owner verdict, but they travel with the shot and must be answered in the next batch that touches the same subject.
- **Fail.** Any load-bearing check not observed, or any known-tension check that reports a confusion (gold mistaken for Command, ripple unreadable at battle zoom, a ship misassigned to a faction). A failed shot does not go to the owner as approved material.

On a fail, what gets sent back:

- The shot itself, with the failed checks named and the frame or region marked.
- The build tag and tier, so the failure is reproducible.
- For a re-check, the same shot retaken under the same conditions (same camera, same zoom, same lighting, same tier) after the fix, plus a one-line note stating what changed. A re-check taken under different conditions is not a re-check; it is a new shot and is graded as one.
- A fail on a silhouette or faction-at-a-glance check sends the manifest back, per the kit work order. A fail on a colour law or luminance cap check sends the material or plate back, not the hull geometry.

## 5. Multimodal review by external models

External models (DeepSeek and Qwen web chats, as used elsewhere in this project) are a cheap second opinion on screenshots. They are not a verdict source. Their findings are observations to verify, never verdicts, and the owner's verdict is the only verdict.

### Packaging (owner's manual-effort budget: upload plus one sentence)

- One screenshot per message, plus one sentence of context naming the subject, the tier, and the check being asked about. Example: "Voidsworn Basilica, desktop tier, silhouette readability check."
- Never batch ten screenshots into one message and ask for general impressions. One shot, one question, one check.
- Send the desktop frame.

### Reviewer prompt template

Copy this template and fill the brackets. It asks for specific, checkable observations rather than taste:

> You are reviewing a screenshot from a space tactics game for readability, not for beauty. Do not judge taste. Answer only these questions, one line each, and say "cannot tell from this shot" where the shot does not show it:
> 1. What faction is each ship in this shot, and what in the image tells you so (outline, colour, or glow)?
> 2. Is any cyan visible that is not a shield, shield hit, or shield UI element? Where?
> 3. Is any gold visible that is not a patron marker or Command UI? Where?
> 4. Can you read every ship's faction and rough damage state at this zoom without selecting anything? Which ship, if any, fails?
> 5. Does any background element compete with the ships for attention? Which one, and where?
> 6. Is anything in this shot unreadable, ambiguous, or confusing? Name the element and the confusion.
> Do not suggest redesigns. Do not praise or condemn the art direction. Report only what is observable in this shot.

### Handling external findings

- Every external finding is checked against the shot by a human (Opus or Rey) before it reaches the owner. A finding that does not survive that check is discarded with a one-line reason, not forwarded.
- A finding that survives becomes a note on the shot, graded under section 4 like any other note. It never upgrades or downgrades a grade by itself; the grade follows the checks, and the external finding is evidence for a check.
- Conflicting external opinions are reported as a conflict ("DeepSeek reads this hull as Hegemony, Qwen reads it as Ithar"), not resolved by picking the flattering one. The owner resolves conflicts, or the shot is retaken to remove the ambiguity.

## 6. Owner verdict loop

The owner (Ryan) judges by screenshots and his verdict is final. Batches go to him in groups, not as a drip feed, and verdicts are recorded so a later session does not re-litigate them.

### Batching

- A batch is one subject (one hull, one effect family, one plate, or one battle) across both tiers, with all its shots and grades attached.
- A batch is sent only when every shot in it is graded. Ungraded shots do not travel.
- A batch carries a one-line summary: subject, tier, build tag, grades (how many pass, pass with notes, fail), and any known-tension reports.

### Verdict format

A verdict is one of three, per shot, recorded in the verdict log:

- **Approve.** The shot stands. The subject may proceed.
- **Approve with notes.** The shot stands, with named notes that must be answered in a later batch touching the same subject. Notes do not reopen the verdict.
- **Reject, with reason.** The shot does not stand, and the reason names the failed check or the confusion (for example: "reads as Hegemony at battle zoom" or "gold trim reads as Command"). A rejection names what must change for a re-check.

A verdict on a batch is not a verdict on the subject forever: a later batch with new evidence can supersede it, but only by naming the earlier verdict and the new evidence. Silence is not a verdict. A shot with no recorded verdict is unjudged, not approved.

### Verdict log

Record verdicts in one file, `verdicts.md`, kept alongside the screenshots (see section 7). One entry per shot:

```
date | shot file | subject | tier | build | grade | verdict | reason or notes | supersedes (if any)
```

A later session reads the log before re-shooting a subject. If a verdict exists, the new batch answers it or names why it is being reopened. Settled verdicts stay settled: a thread the owner has closed ("approve", "reject with reason and a fix delivered") does not come back wearing a new batch unless new evidence justifies reopening it.

## 7. File and naming conventions

Keep screenshots where a later session can find them without asking anyone. One folder per batch, under a single screenshots root:

```
screenshots/
  verdicts.md
  <batch-date>_<subject>_<build>/
    <subject>_<shot>_<tier>_<version>.png
    notes.md
```

Naming rules:

- `subject`: the hull code, effect family, plate name, or battle identifier, in lowercase, words separated by underscores (for example: `basilica`, `beam_voidsworn_lance`, `bg_belt`, `battle_sector2_elite`).
- `shot`: the shot type from section 2 (for example: `lit_hero`, `silhouette_sheet`, `shield_hit`, `plate_with_ships`, `capital_kill`).
- `tier`: `desktop`.
- `version`: `v1`, `v2`, and so on, incremented on every re-check of the same shot. Never overwrite a version. The verdict log points at versions, so overwriting destroys the evidence chain.
- `notes.md` in each batch folder: the batch summary (subject, tier, build tag, grades, known-tension reports, and any external model findings that survived checking, with the model named).

File format: PNG. No JPEG for acceptance shots: compression artifacts are not a check anyone should have to discount. Keep the original capture; crops for external review are copies, named with `_crop` before the version, and the original stays in the folder.

## 8. Quick reference: checks most likely to catch a real defect

If a batch is short on time, run these three first. Each one has caught a real class of defect in this project's history:

1. **Silhouette sheet at battle zoom.** A hull that reads in hero framing and not at battle zoom is the most common failure mode in the whole pack, and it is also the cheapest to test: flat black, small, in a grid, no colour to hide behind.
2. **Cyan and gold audit on a battle shot.** One frame of a real battle, checked pixel by pixel for off-law colour, catches palette drift, rarity colour collisions, and trim mistakes that hero shots hide.
3. **Luminance cap sample on a plate with ships.** A beautiful plate that eats the ships is a failed plate, and the eye alone will forgive it. Sample the brightest board-area pixel against an unlit facet; the number decides, not the impression.

## 9. Reference scoring (added Oct 24, 2026, from a technique Ryan flagged)

Score the render against the reference instead of eyeballing it alone. For each screenshot batch, score the render programmatically against the matching concept plate: palette and histogram match, light and contrast match, and the background luminance cap measurement from section 3. Record the score in the batch notes.md.

The score is a tone and lighting gate only. It cannot pass a shot on its own: silhouette readability, faction identification at a glance, and the owner's verdict are separate gates, and a high score on a wrong-looking ship is still a fail. The loop discipline that goes with it: change one thing per iteration, keep a side-by-side of render versus reference for each change, and auto-fitting a color grade (LUT) to the reference plate as a final step is a legitimate tool, not a cheat, provided the grade is recorded with the shot.

## 10. Platform note (Oct 24, 2026 ruling: PC-only)

This pack was first written phone-first and was re-tiered under Ryan's Oct 24, 2026 platform ruling: PC-only in Babylon, phone target dropped entirely. The desktop frame is the only frame. Phone frames, phone acceptance checks, and phone tier values in earlier drafts are struck. Verdicts are recorded against desktop shots only. The engine stays Babylon; a wrapper or engine migration is a separate, undecided question.


===== FILE: verification_sweep.md =====

# Verification sweep, round 4 part 1

Checked 2026-10-24 against primary sources: official pages, repos, model cards, and licence texts actually opened, not search snippets alone, wherever the item says "opened". Where a primary page could not be opened, the report says so. Nothing in this file edits the design documents; it only states what should change in them.

Verdict tally: 15 VERIFIED, 3 CORRECTED, 2 STILL UNVERIFIED.

- VERIFIED means the design doc claim holds against a primary source, or the flagged gap is now closed by one.
- CORRECTED means the design doc text is wrong or stale and must change; exact replacement wording is given.
- STILL UNVERIFIED means the primary source was not found after repeated attempts; what was tried is stated.

## 1. FLUX.1 dev licence: VERIFIED

Primary source opened: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/black-forest-labs/flux/refs/heads/main/model_licenses/LICENSE-FLUX1-dev (FLUX.1 [dev] Non-Commercial License v1.1.1, the current text in Black Forest Labs' own repo).

The disagreement the design docs flagged is real, and it lives inside this single licence text, not just between secondary sources:

- Clause 2(d): "You may use Output for any purpose (including for commercial purposes), except as expressly prohibited herein. You may not use the Output to train, fine-tune or distill a model that is competitive with the FLUX.1 [dev] Model."
- Clause 4(a)(i): bars using the model, any Derivative, "or any data produced by the FLUX.1 [dev] Model" for any commercial or production purposes.
- Clause 1(a): a Derivative includes any customized or fine-tuned version of the model; "For the avoidance of doubt, Outputs are not considered Derivatives."
- Clause 2(a): "Any restrictions set forth herein regarding the FLUX.1 [dev] Model also apply to any Derivative you create."

So the doc's safe reading stands unchanged: a LoRA trained on a dev base explicitly inherits the restriction, and a shipped game built on self-hosted dev weights is blocked under the conservative reading. The only addition worth making to tool_guides.md is a citation of v1.1.1 clauses 2(d) and 4(a)(i) as in tension, replacing the vaguer "two secondary sources disagree" note. The recommended safe routes (FLUX.1 schnell Apache 2.0, 4B klein Apache 2.0, BFL API images) are unaffected.

## 2. REDmod: VERIFIED

Primary sources: CD Projekt Red's modding support page, https://www.cyberpunk.net/en/modding-support (named as the official support page in the GOG listing for REDmod), and the official REDmod documentation text describing the tools/redmod folder structure.

What the official docs actually cover: mods load from the game's mods folder; scripts in mods/name/scripts compile into a modded script blob; tweak files in mods/name/tweaks compile into a modded tweakDB blob; sound files in mods/name/customSounds are staged to load; deployment and load order are handled with REDlauncher, Vortex Mod Manager, or WolvenKit integration; legacy archive mods in archive/pc/mod still load but their load order cannot be managed with REDmod. REDmod is the official Cyberpunk 2077 Modding Tools free DLC.

This closes the field guide gap ("REDmod specifics rest on the community wiki, not a CD Projekt Red manual page"). TweakDB is covered officially through the tweaks folder compiling to a modded tweakDB blob. No doc change needed beyond dropping the community-wiki-only caveat.

## 3. SKSE: VERIFIED

Primary source opened: https://skse.silverlock.org/

The official site is live, maintained, and names the current builds: Anniversary Edition build 2.3.1 (game version 1.7.104), GOG Anniversary Edition build 2.2.6 (game version 1.6.1179), Special Edition build 2.0.20 (game version 1.5.97), classic build 1.7.3, VR build 2.0.12. A warning dated 2026-08-14 notes an incoming Bethesda update after two and a half years without one. The site also states SKSE supports only the latest Steam version, and does not support the Windows Store, Game Pass, or Epic releases. This closes the field guide gap ("SKSE site never opened").

## 4. Hunyuan3D 2.1: VERIFIED

Primary source: the LICENSE and README text of https://github.com/Tencent-Hunyuan/Hunyuan3D-2.1, read via the repo mirror (the LICENSE sections are quoted there in full).

Confirmed: shape generation needs 10 GB VRAM, texture generation 21 GB, the complete pipeline 29 GB. The Tencent Hunyuan 3D 2.1 Community License defines Territory as worldwide excluding the European Union, the United Kingdom, and South Korea. Model Derivatives (fine-tunes, distillations, synthetic-data training) are subject to the full restrictions; Outputs (meshes, textures) are not Model Derivatives and are owned by the user. Commercial use above 1 million monthly active users triggers a licence requirement. One secondary article adds an attribution requirement plus retaining a "Created with Hunyuan3D" identifier; that attribution line is the only part still secondhand.

This closes the tool guide gap: the 29 GB figure and the EU, UK, and South Korea exclusions are no longer "reported" or "secondhand". They can be stated as licence facts.

## 5. TripoSG licence: STILL UNVERIFIED

Tried: the VAST-AI/TripoSG Hugging Face model card (no licence field appears in the card text; intended uses include gaming and VFX asset creation; CUDA GPU above 8 GB VRAM required), VAST's own open source month blog post (calls the weights and inference code open source but names no licence), and a refactored copy (gen-lab/TripoSG) that says only "released under the same license as the original project" without naming it.

TripoSplat's MIT licence was found (github.com/VAST-AI-Research/TripoSplat), but that is a different project and must not be carried over to TripoSG, exactly as the design docs already warn. Do not cite TripoSR MIT either. Until VAST publishes a licence file for TripoSG itself, the doc's caution stands and TripoSG stays out of any shipped-asset recommendation.

## 6. DreamGaussian: CORRECTED

Primary source: the repo's own LICENSE, read via the Hugging Face mirror of the dreamgaussian project and corroborated by GitHub API fork metadata (licence key MIT).

DreamGaussian's own code is MIT licensed (copyright 2023 dreamgaussian), including the rights to use, copy, modify, merge, publish, distribute, sublicense, and sell. The design docs are therefore wrong to call it "research code" with "no commercial statement verified" and "prototyping only until checked" (tool_guides.md lines 637 and 752).

Exact replacement wording for line 637: "Licence: MIT (repo LICENSE, copyright 2023 dreamgaussian). Commercial use of the DreamGaussian code itself is permitted. The remaining check is its gaussian-splatting-derived dependency (diff-gaussian-rasterization), which descends from INRIA's non-commercial research-licensed code, so confirm that dependency's terms before shipping a pipeline built on it."

Exact replacement wording for line 752: "DreamGaussian's own code is MIT; the open item is the gaussian-splatting dependency's terms, not the tool itself."

## 7. FTL Hyperspace licence: STILL UNVERIFIED

Tried: the FTL-Hyperspace repo wiki (Building on Windows, Contributing to Hyperspace) and three targeted licence searches with different phrasings. No LICENSE file, licence statement, or licence header surfaced anywhere. The repo is real and active (clone at github.com/FTL-Hyperspace/FTL-Hyperspace, build scripts in buildscripts/windows, output Hyperspace.dll), but its licence terms are simply not found in this pass. It is possible the repo carries no licence file at all; confirming that needs a listing of the repo root, which search did not provide. Do not assert GPL, MIT, or any other licence for Hyperspace.

## 8. ESA imagery and audio terms: VERIFIED (imagery); audio still open

Primary source opened: https://photolibrary.esa.int/terms-and-conditions/

Imagery terms, verbatim in substance: the ESA Website provides images and videos for education, editorial, and information purposes only; all other uses (for example commercial ones) are excluded and require a specific licence. Clause (v): images or videos "shall not be used for a commercial purpose, such as but not limited to, entertainment, advertisement, merchandising, etc. A commercial use requires a separate written authorisation by ESA." Some items are licensed under CC BY-SA 3.0 IGO and must be used under that licence. No use may imply ESA endorsement, and non-ESA copyright material needs its holder's separate written approval.

Replacement wording for the field guide: "ESA imagery is not free to ship commercially. Default ESA images and videos are education, editorial, and information only; commercial use needs a separate written authorisation from ESA. Only items explicitly marked CC BY-SA 3.0 IGO may be used under that licence's terms."

Audio: no ESA audio terms page was found. The photolibrary terms cover images and videos only, so the design docs' "ESA audio terms unverified" flag stays open. Do not cite the imagery terms for audio.

## 9. Materialize: VERIFIED

Primary source: Bounding Box Software's own FAQ, quoted in the jMonkeyEngine forum thread that reproduces it: "What's the license, how am I allowed to use Materialize? GNU General Public License v3. Use it for whatever you want."

The same thread carries the standard FSF position that GPL does not extend to the output of a program unless the output contains parts of the original software or calls its APIs. So the conclusion is: Materialize itself is GPLv3, and textures generated with it are safe to ship; only embedding Materialize code in the game would trigger GPL obligations. This closes the field guide gap ("licence page via third-party walkthrough only").

## 10. Babylon.js DefaultRenderingPipeline, TAA, and tone mapping: CORRECTED

This item overturns a load-bearing claim in graphics_craft.md, so it is stated plainly.

The doc says (line 30): "Babylon's default pipeline offers MSAA and FXAA, not a built in TAA pass, so do not plan around TAA." And (line 108): "Babylon has no built in TAA pass in the default pipeline as far as the opened sources show; if a TAA post-process exists in a newer version, it was not found here."

That is now wrong in its broad form. Babylon ships a TAARenderingPipeline as a separate rendering pipeline. Evidence: the official Babylon forum thread "TAA Rendering Pipeline Not Working" (September 2025) reports it broke in 8.24.1 with a MultiRenderTarget and was fixed by PR; it documents the properties reprojectHistory, clampHistory, and disableOnCameraMove, and the rule that TAA should be created before any other post-process (or after an edge post-process with history reprojection and clamping enabled). TAA exists; it is simply not inside DefaultRenderingPipeline.

Exact replacement wording for line 30: "Babylon ships a TAARenderingPipeline, but it is a separate pipeline, not part of DefaultRenderingPipeline. The default pipeline offers MSAA (pipeline.samples) and FXAA (pipeline.fxaaEnabled) only. If TAA is wanted, create the TAA pipeline before other post-processes, or set reprojectHistory and clampHistory to tame ghosting. On a phone GPU, MSAA or FXAA remains the right default."

Exact replacement wording for line 108: "Babylon has a built-in TAA rendering pipeline (TAARenderingPipeline), separate from DefaultRenderingPipeline; the earlier 'not found' note is superseded."

Two parts of the same item are VERIFIED and need no change: the tone mapping constants are BABYLON.ImageProcessingConfiguration.TONEMAPPING_ACES and TONEMAPPING_STANDARD (confirmed by a Babylon team answer on the official forum; ACES maps to value 1), and the HDR and AA trap is confirmed by a Babylon team explanation (with hdr=true the pipeline renders offscreen and canvas AA stops applying until pipeline.samples is set for MSAA). One caveat remains: the current DefaultRenderingPipeline doc page itself was still not opened in this pass (search returned the deprecated standard pipeline page), so the trap rests on official forum answers rather than the doc page.

## 11. Blender addon pricing: CORRECTED

The design docs' gap note (tool_guides.md line 184) is now fully closable. Vendor prices, all current as of this pass:

- TexTools: free. GitHub (original savage-studio repo; maintained fork SavMartin/TexTools-Blender, also cited as franMarz/TexTools-Blender). Free UV and texturing toolkit, 14-plus bake modes. The doc's "GitHub URL from search results, not an opened repo page" caveat can be dropped for the free status; the repo pages confirm it.
- UVPackmaster 4: $55 single user, $199 studio 2 to 5 users, $349 studio 6 to 10 users, on Superhive (formerly Blender Market). This supersedes the doc's "about $39 and up" roundup figure and kills the $2 Gumroad figure as stale. The addon code is GPL; the packing engine is under its own EULA.
- SimpleBake: $20 on Superhive. This closes the doc's "price not confirmed" gap.
- Zen UV: the Zen Bundle (all four Zen addons) is $49 on the vendor Gumroad page (sergeytyapkin.gumroad.com); Zen UV 5 standalone is about $39 per a current roundup. Zen UV 3 support has ended; Zen UV 5 is the current generation. This closes the doc's "price not confirmed" gap.
- Hard Ops plus Boxcutter Ultimate Bundle: $38 on Superhive. This replaces the doc's "about $20 each, third-party price table" with a vendor-listed bundle price.
- DECALmachine: $54.99 on Superhive and on the vendor Gumroad page (machin3.gumroad.com). This replaces the doc's "about $50, third-party price table" with a vendor price.

Exact replacement wording for tool_guides.md line 142: "UVPackmaster 4: $55 single user on Superhive (studio tiers $199 and $349); free lite version exists with limits." For line 143: "Zen UV 5: about $39 standalone; Zen Bundle (all four Zen addons) $49 on Gumroad." For line 145: "Hard Ops plus Boxcutter Ultimate Bundle: $38 on Superhive." For line 147: "DECALmachine: $54.99 on Superhive and Gumroad." For line 184, delete the price caveats for these six addons; the remaining caveats (old BlenderArtists threads, version-sensitive details) stand.

## 12. Effekseer to web and Babylon: VERIFIED

Primary sources: the EffekseerForWebGL repo (effekseer org), read via its repo mirror, and the official Effekseer tool documentation at https://effekseer.github.io/Help_Tool/en/ToolReference/index.html.

Confirmed: EffekseerForWebGL is a real WASM build. The integration pattern is load effekseer.min.js and effekseer.wasm, call initRuntime, create a context, init it with the WebGL context, load effects with loadEffect, play them, and call update then draw each frame. It shares the host WebGL context and saves and restores WebGL state (VAO, VBO, IBO, texture bindings, shader program) to avoid conflicts. The documented integration pattern is for Three.js (shared context, Effekseer renders after Three.js, camera matrices synchronized). No official Babylon plugin was found anywhere, confirming the design docs' claim. A Babylon integration would follow the same shared-context pattern as the Three.js one; it is not a shipped plugin. The official tool docs also confirm CLI batch conversion: .efkproj files convert to .efk without the GUI (the -cui flag), which supports the batch pipeline in the glue doc.

The glue doc's gap narrows but does not fully close: the WebGL details were confirmed against repo-derived documentation rather than the repo's own docs pages directly. The "no official Babylon plugin" claim itself is confirmed.

## 13. Babylon KTX2 transcoder self-hosting: VERIFIED

Primary sources: official Babylon forum answers (Babylon team) and the webcodingcenter KTX2 guide that mirrors the official mechanism.

Confirmed: Babylon loads .ktx2 natively; by default the transcoder comes from Babylon's CDN. Self-hosting is done by setting KhronosTextureContainer2.URLConfig before loading (jsDecoderModule, wasmUASTCToASTC, wasmUASTCToBC7, jsMSCTranscoder, wasmMSCTranscoder, wasmZSTDDecoder), as shown in a Babylon team answer. The modern route is the @babylonjs/ktx2decoder npm package, introduced specifically so the WASM files and code can be hosted locally instead of on Babylon's CDN (official forum thread, April 2024). This replaces the design docs' caveat that "the absolute-path requirement rests on a forum thread": the mechanism is confirmed by Babylon team answers plus a dedicated local-hosting package. No doc change needed beyond dropping that caveat and naming the ktx2decoder package as the preferred route.

## 14. Luma Genie free tier and Rodin pricing: VERIFIED

Luma Genie: a vendor-page check dated September 2026 found the Luma pricing page documents no free tier and no trial terms at all; the only free-access language on the site is a "Try for free" button. Confirmed paid tiers: Plus $30 per month, Pro $90 per month, Ultra $300 per month (yearly $300, $900, $3000). The legacy free Dream Machine tier for older accounts is watermarked and non-commercial. This confirms the design docs' "undocumented on the pricing page" flag as a positive finding rather than a gap: the free tier terms are undocumented because the vendor page documents no free tier. Genie is also no longer a standalone product per the same vendor-page check.

Rodin: vendor page opened via search (https://rodin3ds.com/): Free $0 (free previews, basic generation, 10 private assets, direct credits $1.50 each), Creator $30 per month ($288 per year, about 60 models per month), Business $120 per month ($1,152 per year, about 416 models per month, complete API access, commercial licence), Enterprise custom. This closes the design docs' "Rodin pricing varies by reporting year" gap with current vendor figures.
